1c:[["$","p",null,{}],"\n",["$","blockquote",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","em",null,{"children":"Thou shalt have the power to degenerate"}],"\n—",["$","em",null,{"children":"On the Dignity of Man,"}]," 1496"]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","h2",null,{"id":"introduction","children":"Introduction"}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","em",null,{"children":"Singular learning theory (SLT)"}]," is a theory of learning that accounts for ",["$","em",null,{"children":"degeneracy"}]," in neural networks. What is degeneracy? Let us first appeal to the Cambridge dictionary, documenting some features of typical uses of the word within mathematics:"]}],"\n",["$","blockquote",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","em",null,{"children":"Degenerate"}]," (of an equation, curve, line, etc.) unusual or complicated in some way compared to other equations, curves, lines, etc.  of a similar type, especially because a variable or parameter is zero."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["On the other hand, we should recall that deep learning has as many roots in neuroscience as in mathematics. And as they say, \"neural networks are ",["$","em",null,{"children":"grown."}],"\" Perhaps we should also hear the biologist's definition:"]}],"\n",["$","blockquote",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","em",null,{"children":"Degenerate"}]," (of an organism, chromosome, etc.) simpler than a form that previously existed because of no longer having a particular structure."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["Actually, ",["$","em",null,{"children":"both"}]," of these definitions are apt. Within a typical neural network architecture, certain weight vectors correspond to neural networks from simpler architectures (often due to certain weights being zero). Structurally, these neural networks are simpler in form than their neighbours. Mathematically, they complicate the relationship between the neural network's parameter space and the resulting space of functions. In turn, learning in neural networks is substantially richer than learning in classical statistical models."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"Singular learning theory is a framework for understanding learning that places degeneracy at the centre. The framework leverages powerful tools from algebraic geometry to understand, characterise, and resolve degeneracies, revealing their impact on learning. Unfortunately, the price of this mathematical power is that following the research literature without a deep background in pure mathematics is challenging."}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"That is where this tutorial comes in. We aim to distil the essence of singular learning theory—the notion of degeneracy and its role in learning—laying bare through simple examples and structured exercises the core intuitions driving research in the field. We believe understanding this essence requires no more than undergraduate-level mathematics. Moreover, to anyone armed with these core intuitions, understanding and contributing to research on the relationship between degeneracy and deep learning is within reach."}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Contents."}],"  Specifically, the tutorial comprises the following four technical sections, each with definitions and guided exercises."]}],"\n",["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\"",["$","a",null,{"href":"#1-preliminaries","children":"Section 1"}],"\" reviews core deep learning concepts, defining our notation and some running examples. The central concept is that of the parameter–function map."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\"",["$","a",null,{"href":"#2-what-is-degeneracy","children":"Section 2"}],"\" defines degeneracy of parameter–function maps and relates it to symmetries, singularities of the Fisher information, and degenerate critical points of the loss landscape."]}],"\n","$L1d","\n","$L1e","\n"]}],"\n","$L1f","\n","$L20","\n","$L21","\n","$L22","\n","$L23","\n","$L24","\n","$L25","\n","$L26","\n","$L27","\n","$L28","\n","$L29","\n","$L2a","\n","$L2b","\n","$L2c","\n","$L2d","\n","$L2e","\n","$L2f","\n","$L30","\n","$L31","\n","$L32","\n","$L33","\n","$L34","\n","$L35","\n","$L36","\n","$L37","\n","$L38","\n","$L39","\n","$L3a","\n","$L3b","\n","$L3c","\n","$L3d","\n","$L3e","\n","$L3f","\n","$L40","\n","$L41","\n","$L42","\n","$L43","\n","$L44","\n","$L45","\n","$L46","\n","$L47","\n","$L48","\n","$L49","\n","$L4a","\n","$L4b","\n","$L4c","\n","$L4d","\n","$L4e","\n","$L4f","\n","$L50","\n","$L51","\n","$L52","\n","$L53","\n","$L54","\n","$L55","\n","$L56","\n","$L57","\n","$L58","\n","$L59","\n","$L5a","\n","$L5b","\n","$L5c","\n","$L5d","\n","$L5e","\n","$L5f","\n","$L60","\n","$L61","\n","$L62","\n","$L63","\n","$L64","\n","$L65","\n","$L66","\n","$L67","\n","$L68","\n","$L69","\n","$L6a","\n","$L6b","\n","$L6c","\n","$L6d","\n","$L6e","\n","$L6f","\n","$L70","\n","$L71","\n","$L72","\n","$L73","\n","$L74","\n","$L75","\n","$L76","\n","$L77","\n","$L78","\n","$L79","\n","$L7a","\n","$L7b","\n","$L7c","\n","$L7d","\n","$L7e","\n","$L7f","\n","$L80","\n","$L81","\n","$L82","\n","$L83","\n","$L84","\n","$L85","\n","$L86","\n","$L87","\n","$L88","\n","$L89","\n","$L8a","\n","$L8b","\n","$L8c","\n","$L8d","\n","$L8e","\n","$L8f","\n","$L90","\n","$L91","\n","$L92","\n","$L93","\n","$L94","\n","$L95","\n","$L96","\n","$L97","\n","$L98","\n","$L99","\n","$L9a","\n","$L9b","\n","$L9c","\n","$L9d","\n","$L9e","\n","$L9f","\n","$La0","\n","$La1","\n","$La2","\n","$La3","\n","$La4","\n","$La5","\n","$La6","\n","$La7","\n","$La8","\n","$La9","\n","$Laa","\n","$Lab","\n","$Lac","\n","$Lad","\n","$Lae","\n","$Laf","\n","$Lb0","\n","$Lb1","\n","$Lb2","\n","$Lb3","\n","$Lb4","\n","$Lb5","\n","$Lb6","\n","$Lb7","\n","$Lb8","\n","$Lb9","\n","$Lba","\n","$Lbb","\n","$Lbc","\n","$Lbd","\n","$Lbe","\n","$Lbf","\n","$Lc0","\n","$Lc 47:["$","h3",null,{"id":"12-supervised-deep-learning-and-loss-functions","children":"1.2 Supervised deep learning and loss functions"}]
48:["$","p",null,{"children":["In a typical supervised deep learning setting, we are given a data set of pairs ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\((x^{(1)}, y^{(1)}),\\)"}}]," ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\ldots,\\)"}}]," ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\((x^{(n)}, y^{(n)}) \\in \\X \\times \\Y\\)"}}],". These pairs exemplify inputs and their corresponding (possibly noisy) outputs from an unknown target function ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(f : \\X \\to \\Y\\)"}}]," which we would like to approximate using a neural network. That is, we would like to find a parameter ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(w \\in \\W\\)"}}]," such that the corresponding function ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(f_{w}\\)"}}]," approximates the target function ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(f\\)"}}],"."]}]
49:["$","p",null,{"children":["Formally, given an example ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\((x, y) \\in \\X \\times \\Y\\)"}}],", define a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"per-example loss"}]," function ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(L_{x,y}: \\W \\to \\R\\)"}}]," to measure the deviation of ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(f_{w}(x)\\)"}}]," from the output ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(y\\)"}}],". A typical loss function for vector outputs ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Y \\subset \\R^{m}\\)"}}]," is to use the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"squared error"}]," loss"]}]
4a:["$","div",null,{"id":"eq-square-loss","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[L_{x,y}(w) = \\|f_{w}(x) - y\\|^{2}.\\]"}}]}]
4b:["$","p",null,{"children":["If ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Y = \\Delta^{m-1}\\)"}}]," is a space of discrete distributions over ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(m\\)"}}]," objects it is typical to use ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"cross entropy"}]," loss"]}]
4c:["$","div",null,{"id":"eq-cross-entropy","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[L_{x,y}(w) = - \\sum_{i=1}^{m} y(i) \\log f_{w}(i \\mid x).\\]"}}]}]
4d:["$","p",null,{"children":["Given a per-example loss function and a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"data distribution"}]," ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(q \\in \\Delta(\\X\\times\\Y)\\)"}}]," over the space of examples (representing our possibly-noisy target function), we formalise the objective of supervised learning as finding ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(w \\in \\W\\)"}}]," so as to minimise the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"population loss"}]," function ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(L : \\W \\to \\R\\)"}}]," defined such that"]}]
4e:["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[L(w) = \\E_{(x,y) \\sim q}[L_{x,y}(w)].\\]"}}]
4f:["$","p",null,{"children":["The population loss aggregates differences on outputs for individual inputs into an overall measure of difference between ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(f_{w}\\)"}}]," and the target function."]}]
50:["$","p",null,{"children":["In practice, we often can't evaluate the population loss over the entire input/output space. 55:["$","details",null,{"suppressHydrationWarning":true,"className":"my-3 rounded-md border border-zinc-200 px-3 py-2","children":[["$","summary",null,{"className":"cursor-pointer font-medium","children":"Solution"}],["$","div",null,{"className":"mt-2","children":[["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["Since the data pairs ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\((x^{(i)}, y^{(i)})\\)"}}]," are sampled independently and identically from ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(q\\)"}}],", each per-example loss ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(L_{x^{(i)},y^{(i)}}(w)\\)"}}]," is an identically distributed random variable with expectation"]}],"\n"]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[\\E_{(x,y)\\sim q}[L_{x,y}(w)] = L(w).\\]"}}],["$","p",null,{"children":"Therefore, by linearity of expectation,"}],["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[\\E[L_{n}(w)] = \\E\\left[\\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^{n} L_{x^{(i)},y^{(i)}}(w)\\right] = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^{n} \\E[L_{x^{(i)},y^{(i)}}(w)] = \\frac{1}{n}\\cdot n \\cdot L(w) = L(w).\\]"}}],["$","ol",null,{"start":"2","children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["The random variables ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(L_{x^{(1)},y^{(1)}}(w), L_{x^{(2)},y^{(2)}}(w), \\ldots\\)"}}]," are independent and identically distributed with common mean ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(L(w)\\)"}}],". 58:["$","h3",null,{"id":"13-statistical-models-and-parameterdistribution-maps","children":"1.3 Statistical models and parameter–distribution maps"}]
59:["$","p",null,{"children":"The previous sections introduce neural networks and supervised deep learning within a framework of function approximation. An alternative approach is to view supervised learning as statistical inference, and particularly Bayesian inference. This statistical framework is commonly used within the SLT literature, and we will encounter it within this tutorial."}]
5a:["$","p",null,{"children":"The first step to upgrading our framework is to move from talking about functions to talking about conditional distributions. For each neural network, we should be able to determine not just the output corresponding to each input, but a probability density measuring how likely each output is given each input."}]
5b:["$","p",null,{"children":["Formally, let ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\D \\subseteq \\Delta(\\Y)^{\\X}\\)"}}]," represent a class of conditional distributions over ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Y\\)"}}],". A distributional neural network architecture comprises a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"parameter–distribution map"}]," ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Psi : \\W \\to \\D\\)"}}],". It is sometimes convenient to refer to the conditional distribution ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Psi(w) : \\X \\to \\Delta(\\Y)\\)"}}]," using the notation ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(p_{w} : \\X \\to \\Delta(\\Y)\\)"}}],". The probability mass/density of a given output ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(y \\in \\Y\\)"}}]," conditional on a given input ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(x \\in \\X\\)"}}]," is typically denoted either ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(p_{w}(y \\mid x)\\)"}}]," or ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(p(y \\mid x, w)\\)"}}],"."]}]
5c:["$","p",null,{"children":["Sometimes, defining a parameter–distribution map is simply a re-framing. For example, in image classification, our output space ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Y\\)"}}]," was a space of distributions over image classes. Let ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\C\\)"}}]," represents the underlying class space, then ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Y = \\Delta(\\C)\\)"}}]," and we can view our parameter–function map ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Phi : \\W \\to \\F\\)"}}]," as a parameter–distribution map ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Psi : \\W \\to \\D\\)"}}]," with ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\D = \\F \\subseteq \\Y^{\\X} = \\Delta(\\C)^{\\X}\\)"}}],"."]}]
5d:["$","p",null,{"children":["In other cases, we can easily construct a parameter–distribution map from a parameter–function map by introducing a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"noise model"}],". For example, in regression with an output space ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Y = \\R^{m}\\)"}}],", given a parameter–function map ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(w \\mapsto f_{w}\\)"}}]," we can define a parameter–distribution map ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Psi : \\W \\to \\Delta(\\Y)^{\\X}\\)"}}]," such that, for ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(w\\in\\W\\)"}}]," and ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(x\\in\\X\\)"}}],","]}]
5e:["$","div",null,{"id":"eq-gaussian-model","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[p(y \\mid x, w) = \\frac{1}{(2\\pi)^{m/2}}\\exp\\!\\left(-\\frac{\\|y - f_{w}(x)\\|^{2}}{2}\\right).\\]"}}]}]
5f:["$","p",null,{"children":["The above ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Gaussian noise model"}]," amounts to modelling each output ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(y\\)"}}]," as drawn given ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(x, w\\)"}}]," according to the rule ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(y = f_{w}(x) + \\varepsilon\\)"}}]," with ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\varepsilon \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, I_{m})\\)"}}],"."]}]

62:["$","details",null,{"suppressHydrationWarning":true,"className":"my-3 rounded-md border border-zinc-200 px-3 py-2","children":[["$","summary",null,{"className":"cursor-pointer font-medium","children":"Solution"}],["$","div",null,{"className":"mt-2","children":[["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Since labels are sampled independently, the likelihood factorises as a product of Gaussian densities:"}],"\n"]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[\\begin{aligned}p(Y_{n} \\mid X_{n}, w)&amp;= \\prod_{i=1}^{n} p(y^{(i)}| x^{(i)}, w) \\\\&amp;= \\prod_{i=1}^{n} \\frac{1}{(2\\pi)^{m/2}}\\exp\\!\\left( -\\frac{\\|y^{(i)}- f_{w}(x^{(i)})\\|^{2}}{2}\\right).\\end{aligned}\\]"}}],["$","ol",null,{"start":"2","children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Taking the negative logarithm of the above expression,"}],"\n"]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[\\begin{aligned}-\\log p(Y_{n} \\mid X_{n}, w)&amp;= -\\sum_{i=1}^{n} \\left[ -\\frac{m}{2}\\log(2\\pi) - 64:["$","details",null,{"suppressHydrationWarning":true,"className":"my-3 rounded-md border border-zinc-200 px-3 py-2","children":[["$","summary",null,{"className":"cursor-pointer font-medium","children":"Solution"}],["$","div",null,{"className":"mt-2","children":[["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Since labels are sampled independently, the likelihood factorises as"}],"\n"]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[p(C_{n} \\mid X_{n}, w) = \\prod_{i=1}^{n} p(c^{(i)}| x^{(i)}, w) = \\prod_{i=1}^{n} f_{w}(c^{(i)}\\mid x^{(i)}).\\]"}}],["$","ol",null,{"start":"2","children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Taking the negative logarithm,"}],"\n"]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[-\\log p(C_{n} \\mid X_{n}, w) = -\\sum_{i=1}^{n} \\log f_{w}(c^{(i)}\\mid x^{(i)}).\\]"}}],["$","ol",null,{"start":"3","children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["Since ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(y^{(i)}= \\delta_{c^{(i)}}\\)"}}]," is the Dirac distribution on class ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(c^{(i)}\\)"}}],", we have ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(y^{(i)}(j) = 1\\)"}}]," if ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(j = c^{(i)}\\)"}}]," and ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(y^{(i)}(j) = 0\\)"}}]," otherwise. 65:["$","section",null,{"id":"ex-nll-energy","className":"my-6 border border-orange-300 bg-orange-50 rounded-md p-4","children":[["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Exercise 1.5 (Maximum likelihood as loss minimisation, in general)."}]," Consider a neural network architecture with a parameter space ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\W\\)"}}],", parameter–function map ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Phi\\)"}}],", and output space ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\Y = \\R^{m}\\)"}}],". 67:["$","div",null,{"id":"$undefined","className":"my-4 rounded-md border-l-4 px-4 py-3 border-sky-500 bg-sky-50","children":["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Remark (Negative log-likelihood loss)."}]," Examples ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ex-nll-mse","children":"1.3"}],", ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ex-nll-cross-entropy","children":"1.4"}],", and ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ex-nll-energy","children":"1.5"}]," nominally show that some maximum likelihood estimation problems have the same sets of ideal solutions as some carefully-chosen optimisation problems (",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\argmin = \\argmax\\)"}}],"). In general, that the global optima for two optimisation problems coincide is insufficient to show that practical optimisation methods will always find the same solutions: practical optimisation methods don't always find global optima. However, in the above cases, your derivation likely reveals a stronger result: the optimisation landscapes for the function approximation problem are related to the optimisation landscape for the maximum likelihood estimation problem by a strictly monotonically decreasing transform (an affine transform of a logarithm)."]}]}]

69:["$","p",null,{"children":["The principle of maximum likelihood, explored in the preceding exercises, is one approach to statistical inference. An alternative approach is ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Bayesian inference"}],", which treats ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(w\\)"}}]," as a random variable and maintains a probability distribution over ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\W\\)"}}]," reflecting our uncertainty about which parameter best explains the data. 6a:["$","p",null,{"children":["Bayesian learning begins with a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"prior"}]," distribution ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\varphi \\in \\Delta(\\W)\\)"}}],", a probability density on ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\W\\)"}}]," representing our beliefs about the parameter ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(w\\)"}}]," before observing any data. 6c:["$","p",null,{"children":["The posterior re-weights the prior by the likelihood of the observed data: parameters ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(w\\)"}}]," under which the data is more probable receive higher posterior density, while parameters under which the data is improbable are down-weighted."]}]
6d:["$","p",null,{"children":["The partition function ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(Z_{n}\\)"}}]," is the marginal probability of observing the data set, averaged over all parameters weighted by the prior. 70:["$","p",null,{"children":["The ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Bayesian deep learning process"}]," is the process of re-weighting each individual parameter vector in response to seeing an increasing amount of data, producing the sequence of belief distributions ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\varphi, \\pi_{1}, \\pi_{2}, \\ldots \\in \\Delta(\\W)\\)"}}],". Over time, the posterior will concentrate around parameters which provide a good fit for the data (though there is more to the story in the singular case, as we will see in later sections)."]}]
71:["$","p",null,{"children":["Bayesian deep learning is a ",["$","em",null,{"children":"global search"}]," in that all parameter vectors are considered in parallel. This makes exact Bayesian learning computationally intractable for non-trivial models, but more analytically tractable than SGD in general. Moreover, as we will explore in later sections, the dynamics of Bayesian learning still reflect the local geometry of the parameter–distribution map."]}]

77:["$","section",null,{"id":"ex-partition-decomposition","className":"my-6 border border-orange-300 bg-orange-50 rounded-md p-4","children":[["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Exercise 1.8 (Partition function of partitioned parameter space)."}]," Let ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\U_{1}, \\ldots, \\U_{k} \\subseteq \\W\\)"}}]," be disjoint subsets with ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\W = \\U_{1} \\sqcup \\cdots \\sqcup \\U_{k}\\)"}}],"."]}],["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"(a)"}]," Show that the partition function decomposes as ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(Z_{n} = \\sum_{j=1}^{k} Z_{n}(\\U_{j})\\)"}}],", and hence"]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[F_{n} = -\\log \\sum_{j=1}^{k} e^{-F_n(\\U_j)}.\\]"}}],["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"(b)"}]," Show that if ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(F_{n}(\\U_{1}) &lt; F_{n}(\\U_{j})\\)"}}]," for all ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(j \\neq 1\\)"}}],", then"]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[F_{n}(\\U_{1}) - \\log k &lt; F_{n} &lt; F_{n}(\\U_{1}).\\]"}}],["$","p",null,{"children":"In conclusion, the overall free energy is dominated by the region(s) with the lowest local free energy."}]]}]
78:["$","details",null,{"suppressHydrationWarning":true,"className":"my-3 rounded-md border border-zinc-200 px-3 py-2","children":[["$","summary",null,{"className":"cursor-pointer font-medium","children":"Solution"}],["$","div",null,{"className":"mt-2","children":[["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["Since ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\U_{1}, \\ldots, \\U_{k}\\)"}}]," are disjoint and cover ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\W\\)"}}],", the integral over ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\W\\)"}}]," decomposes as a sum of integrals over each ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\U_{j}\\)"}}],":"]}],"\n"]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"math-display","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\[\\begin{aligned}Z_{n}&amp;= \\int_{\\W}\\varphi(w) \\exp\\!\\big({-}n L_{n}(w)\\big)\\, dw \\\\&amp;= \\sum_{j=1}^{k}\\int_{\\U_j}\\varphi(w) \\exp\\!\\big({-}n L_{n}(w)\\big)\\, dw = \\sum_{j=1}^{k}Z_{n}(\\U_{j}).\\end{aligned}\\]"}}],["$","p",null,{"children":["By definition, ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(Z_{n}(\\U_{j}) = e^{-F_n(\\U_j)}\\)"}}],", so ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(Z_{n} = \\sum_{j=1}^{k}e^{-F_n(\\U_j)}\\)"}}],". 8f:["$","p",null,{"children":"A common source of degeneracy in neural network architectures is the presence of continuous symmetries of the parameter–function map. A continuous symmetry traces out a curve of functionally equivalent parameters. The tangent to this curve is a degenerate direction. In this section, we will explore this kind of symmetry and some examples from deep learning."}]

98:["$","details",null,{"suppressHydrationWarning":true,"className":"my-3 rounded-md border border-zinc-200 px-3 py-2","children":[["$","summary",null,{"className":"cursor-pointer font-medium","children":"Proof"}],["$","div",null,{"className":"mt-2","children":[["$","p",null,{"children":["Define the curve in parameter space traced by the continuous symmetry, ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\gamma : \\R \\to \\W\\)"}}]," with ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\gamma(t) = T_{t}(w)\\)"}}],". a0:["$","p",null,{"children":"We have seen that a non-trivial continuous symmetry traces out curves of functionally equivalent parameters throughout the entire parameter space, and the tangent directions to these curves are degenerate directions at every point. ac:["$","div",null,{"id":"$undefined","className":"my-4 rounded-md border-l-4 px-4 py-3 border-sky-500 bg-sky-50","children":["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Remark (Measure zero degenerate sets)."}]," When a parameter–function map is degenerate somewhere but not everywhere, it is often the case that it is degenerate only within a measure-zero subset of parameter space. This means that sampling parameters uniformly at random results in a degenerate parameter with probability zero. However, this does not mean that the degeneracies can be dismissed. The learning process applies a non-random selection pressure and may select parameters from a measure-zero set. Moreover, the existence of degenerate parameters can have practical consequences for nearby non-degenerate parameters, and the collective neighbourhoods of all degenerate parameters comprise a non-measure-zero subset of parameter space."]}]}]

df:["$","details",null,{"suppressHydrationWarning":true,"className":"my-3 rounded-md border border-zinc-200 px-3 py-2","children":[["$","summary",null,{"className":"cursor-pointer font-medium","children":"Solution"}],["$","div",null,{"className":"mt-2","children":[["$","p",null,{"children":["Because ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(w^{*}\\)"}}]," is a local minimum, the gradient ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\nabla L(w^{*})\\)"}}]," is zero. However, the minimum of ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(L\\)"}}]," is at ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\mu = \\mu_{0} = 0.1\\)"}}],", very close to the singularity at ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(\\mu = 0\\)"}}]," where ","$L1ea",". For moderate ","$L1eb",", the posterior is spread broadly enough that it \"feels\" the nearby singularity, and ","$L1ec"," behaves more like the ","$L1ed"," regime from part (a). Only for sufficiently large ","$L1ee"," does the posterior concentrate tightly enough around ","$L1ef"," for the regular ","$L1f0"," asymptotics to take hold. This demonstrates that the singularity at ","$L1f1"," influences learning even when ","$L1f2",", provided ","$L1f3"," is close to the singular point."]}],"\n"]}]]}]]}]

140:["$","p",null,{"children":["Over the last few years, a community of researchers have pursued the application of SLT to advancing the science and safety of deep learning. 141:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Characterising and estimating degeneracy in practice."}],"  We have seen examples of parameter–function map degeneracy in simple neural networks. Symmetries of neural network parameter–function maps have long been studied, however often the emphasis has been on discrete or globally continuous symmetries rather than additional symmetries localised to subsets of parameter space (see ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-farrugiaroberts2022","children":"Farrugia-Roberts 2022"}],", § 2.3, for a survey). For two-layer hyperbolic tangent networks, ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-farrugiaroberts2024","children":"Farrugia-Roberts 2024"}]," characterises the regions of parameter space which display additional degeneracy, and ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-farrugiaroberts2023","children":"Farrugia-Roberts 2023"}]," characterises degenerate directions in the parameter–function map."]}]
142:["$","p",null,{"children":["As discussed, analytically calculating the (local) learning coefficient for deep neural networks is challenging. However, precise formulas have been derived for multi-layer deep linear networks (",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-aoyagi-watanabe2005","children":"Aoyagi & Watanabe 2005"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-aoyagi2024","children":"Aoyagi 2024"}],"), two-layer hyperbolic tangent networks (",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-aoyagi2009","children":"Aoyagi 2009"}],"), and certain other architectures. These results assume data is generated from a known \"teacher\" model."]}]
143:["$","p",null,{"children":["In practice, we lack knowledge of the true data generating process, and we use more complex architectures. Much work has therefore built on the foundational methods of estimating the local learning coefficient with scalable Markov chain Monte Carlo methods (",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-lau-2025","children":"Lau et al. 2025"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-hitchcock-hoogland2025","children":"Hitchcock & Hoogland 2025"}],"). 144:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Bayesian phase transitions and stagewise development."}],"  As we have discussed, Watanabe's free energy formula suggests Bayesian deep learning should undergo Bayesian phase transitions under certain conditions. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-carroll2021","children":"Carroll 2021"}]," studied Bayesian phase transitions in small ReLU networks, and ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-chen-2023","children":"Chen et al. 2023"}]," studied Bayesian phase transitions in a small feature autoencoder (the \"toy model of superposition\" from ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-elhage-2022","children":"Elhage et al. 2022"}],")."]}]
145:["$","p",null,{"children":["In practice, we use stochastic gradient-based optimisation, rather than Bayesian learning, to train neural networks. However, the Bayesian case serves as a model system from which we can derive empirically testable predictions. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-chen-2023","children":"Chen et al. 2023"}]," formulate the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"Bayesian antecedent hypothesis"}],", the empirical conjecture that observed phase transitions in trained neural networks correspond to Bayesian phase transitions modelled by Watanabe's free energy formula. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-chen-2023","children":"Chen et al. 2023"}]," study such ",["$","em",null,{"children":"dynamical phase transitions"}]," in their toy autoencoder and observe a temporal correspondence between phase changes and estimated LLC increases consistent with the free energy formula."]}]
146:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-wang-2024","children":"Wang et al. 2024"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-hoogland-2025","children":"Hoogland et al. 2025"}]," scale this methodology to transformers trained on natural language, finding similar ",["$","em",null,{"children":"stagewise development"}]," phenomena with changes in behaviour and internal structure accompanied by LLC changes. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-panickssery-vaintrob2023","children":"Panickssery & Vaintrob 2023"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-hoogland-2025","children":"Hoogland et al. 2025"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-carroll-2025","children":"Carroll et al. 2025"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-urdshals-urdshals2025","children":"Urdshals & Urdshals 2025"}]," also study developmental stages in transformers trained on synthetic data. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-elliott-2026","children":"Elliott et al. 2026"}]," extends this study to a case of goal misgeneralisation in deep reinforcement learning."]}]
147:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Degeneracy, interpretability, and patterning."}],"  The LLC provides a single number representing the effective dimensionality of a model. We can derive from the same principles—asymptotic properties of the posterior that reflect degeneracies in the model—more fine-grained tools for probing the internal computational structures of neural networks and how they depend on data."]}]
148:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","em",null,{"children":"Weight- and data-refined LLCs:"}]," ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-wang-2025differentiation","children":"Wang et al. 2025"}]," compute LLCs of individual transformer modules (e.g., different attention heads) or with respect to different subsets of a data set (e.g., natural language versus code). Observing these refined quantities over training reveals modules developing different structures specialising to different kinds of data."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","em",null,{"children":"Susceptibilities:"}]," Drawing inspiration from electromagnetic susceptibilities in physics ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-baker-2025","children":"Baker et al. 2025"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-wang-2025embryology","children":"Wang et al. 2025"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-gordon-2026","children":"Gordon et al. 2026"}]," develop and apply a methodology for computing loss susceptibilities so as to reveal more fine-grained information about how model internals relate to data."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","em",null,{"children":"Bayesian influence functions:"}]," Similarly, drawing inspiration from training data attribution in statistics, ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-kreer-2025","children":"Kreer et al. 2025"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-lee-2025","children":"Lee et al. 2025"}],"; ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-adam-2025","children":"Adam et al. 2025"}]," develop a Bayesian generalisation of classical influence functions that allows the influence functions to be sensitive to higher-order degeneracy in the model."]}],"\n"]}]
149:["$","p",null,{"children":"Weight- and data-refined LLCs are themselves LLCs with a different model or data set, and so the same scalable Markov chain Monte Carlo methods can be used to estimate them as for LLCs. Moreover, like the LLC, susceptibilities and Bayesian influence functions can be approximated as expectations over a localised posterior distribution, and so similar estimation methods can be used for these quantities too."}]

18e:["$","p",null,{"children":["Each independent direction in ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(O(h)\\)"}}]," at the identity gives rise to a one-parameter continuous symmetry and hence a degenerate direction at each ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(W\\)"}}],". These directions are the ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(h \\times h\\)"}}]," skew-symmetric matrices ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(S\\)"}}]," (satisfying ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(S^{\\top} = -S\\)"}}],"), since differentiating ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(R(t)^{\\top} R(t) = I\\)"}}]," at ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(t = 0\\)"}}]," gives ",["$","span",null,{"className":"math-inline","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"\\(R'(0)^{\\top} + R'(0) = 0\\)"}}],".
